Theorem 3.6
.

(Bonahon-Wong
[
BW16a
]
for unmarked surfaces, K.-Quesney
[
KQ19b
]
in general)
Suppose
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a root of unity of odd order
N
, then

(1)

The center of
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
is generated by the Casimir elements
H_{\partial}^{\pm 1}
and
H_{p}^{\pm 1}
together with the image of the Frobenius morphism.

(2)

The PI-dimension of
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
is equal to the PI-dimension of
\mathcal{S}^{red}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
so, when
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma_{g,n},\mathcal{A})
, it is equal to
N^{3g-3+n+|\mathcal{A}|}
.

Let us give the main ingredients of the proof of Theorem
3.6
. The idea is to find a geometric interpretation of the quadratic pair
(K_{\Delta},(\cdot,\cdot)^{WP})
and for this, we will associate to the triangulated marked surface
(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
a double branched covering. Let
\Gamma^{\dagger}\subset\Sigma
be the dual of the one-squeleton of the triangulation. The graph
\Gamma^{\dagger}
has one vertex inside each face and one edge
e^{*}
intersecting once transversally each edge
e
of the triangulation. Denote by
B
the set of its vertices. Let
[\Gamma^{\dagger}]
denotes its Borel-Moore relative homology class in
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{c}(\Sigma\setminus B,\partial\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
and
\phi\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma\setminus B;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
the Poincaré-Lefschetz dual of
[\Gamma^{\dagger}]
sending a class
[\alpha]
to its algebraic intersection with
[\Gamma^{\dagger}]
modulo
2
.

Definition 3.7
.

The covering
\pi:\hat{\Sigma}\rightarrow\Sigma
is the double covering of
\Sigma
branched along
B
defined by
\phi
.

Figure
13
illustrates the covering. Let
\widehat{B}=\pi^{-1}(B)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{A})
. Write
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
.

Figure 13.
On the top, the triangle and its double branched covering. The dashed lines represent the dual graph
\Gamma^{\dagger}
, its edges are the branched cuts and its vertices the branched point of the covering. The covering involution is the central symmetry along the branched point. In the middle, a triangulated once-punctured and the corresponding covering, a twice-punctured genus two surface. At the bottom, the double covering of a genus
g
surface with
s
punctures.

Given
(\Sigma,\mathcal{A})
a marked surface, we now define a skew-symmetric form
(\cdot,\cdot):\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma,\mathcal{A};\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}

Let
c_{1},c_{2}:[0,1]\rightarrow\Sigma
be two cycles in
\mathrm{Z}_{1}(\Sigma,\mathcal{A};\mathbb{Z})
, that is such that for
i=1,2
, either
c_{i}(0)=c_{i}(1)
or
c_{i}(0),c_{i}(1)\in\mathcal{A}
. Further suppose that the images of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
intersect transversally in the interior of
\Sigma
along simple crossings. Let
v\in c_{1}\cap c_{2}\subset\mathring{\Sigma}
be an intersection point and denote by
e_{1},e_{2}\in T_{v}\Sigma
the tangent vectors of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
respectively at the point
v
. We define the sign intersection
\varepsilon(v)=+1
if
(e_{1},e_{2})
is an oriented basis of
T_{v}\Sigma
and
\varepsilon(v)=-1
else. Let
a
be a boundary arc and denote by
S(a)
the set of pairs
(v_{1},v_{2})
of points such that
v_{i}\in c_{i}\cap a
. Note that
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
do not have intersection point in
a
by definition. Given
(v_{1},v_{2})\in S(a)
, we define a sign
\varepsilon(v_{1},v_{2})\in\{\pm 1\}
as follows. Isotope
c_{1}
around
a
to bring
v_{1}
in the same position than
v_{2}
and denote by
c_{1}^{\prime}
the new geometric curve. The isotopy should preserve the transversality condition and should not make appear any new inner intersection points. Then define
e_{1},e_{2}\in T_{v_{2}}\Sigma
the tangent vectors at
v_{2}
of
c_{1}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}
respectively. Define
\varepsilon(v_{1},v_{2})=+1
if
(e_{1},e_{2})
is an oriented basis and
\varepsilon(v_{1},v_{2})=-1
else.

Definition 3.8
.

The relative intersection form
(\cdot,\cdot):\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma,\mathcal{A};\mathbb{Z})^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
is defined by:

(c_{1},c_{2}):=\sum_{a}\sum_{(v_{1},v_{2})\in S(a)}\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon(v_{1},v_{2})+\sum_{v\in c_{1}\cap c_{2}}\varepsilon(v)

An easy computation shows that the value
(c_{1},c_{2})
only depends on the relative homology classes of
c_{1},c_{2}
. Note that when
\mathcal{A}=\emptyset
then
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the usual intersection form.

Now let us consider again a triangulated marked surface
(\Sigma,\mathcal{A})
with double covering
(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
. Denote by
\sigma:\widehat{\Sigma}\to\widehat{\Sigma}
the covering involution and let
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\sigma}(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}};\mathbb{Z})\subset\mathrm{H}_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}};\mathbb{Z})
the submodule of classes
[\alpha]
such that
\sigma_{*}([\alpha])=-[\alpha]
. The relative intersection form on
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}};\mathbb{Z})
restricts to a skew-symmetric map (still denoted by the same letter) on
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\sigma}(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}};\mathbb{Z})
with integral values and

Theorem 3.9
.

(Bonahon-Wong
[
BW16a
]
for unmarked surfaces, K.-Quesney
[
KQ19b
]
in general)
The quadratic pair
(K_{\Delta},(\cdot,\cdot)^{WP})
is isomorphic to
(\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\sigma}(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}};\mathbb{Z}),(\cdot,\cdot))
.

Theorem
3.9
is proved by first noticing that it is trivial for a triangle and then studying the behaviour of the gluing operation for the quadratic pairs. Now Theorem
3.6
appears more naturally. For instance consider an unmarked surface
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma_{g,s},\emptyset)
. The double branched covering
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
is depicted in Figure
13
and is obtained by gluing together
g
pairs of tori which are exchanged by
\sigma
together with
2g-3+s
tori on which the covering the elliptic involution together with two balls having
s
inner punctured exchanged by the involution. From this geometric description, we get a decomposition

(\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\sigma}(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}};\mathbb{Z}),(\cdot,\cdot))\cong\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}^{\oplus g}\oplus\begin{pmatrix}0&2\\
-2&0\end{pmatrix}^{\oplus 2g-3+s}\oplus(0)^{\oplus s},

from which we deduce Theorem
3.6
. The case of marked surfaces is similar though slightly more technical.

Note that the set of closed points of the Poisson torus
\mathcal{X}(\mathrm{H}_{1}^{\sigma}(\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B},\widehat{\mathcal{A}};\mathbb{Z}),(\cdot,\cdot))
can be described geometrically as the set of gauge equivalence of flat connections on a trivial
\mathbb{C}^{*}
bundle over
\widehat{\Sigma}\setminus\widehat{B}
which is equivariant in the sense that its holonomy along an arc
\alpha
is the inverse of the holonomy along
\sigma(\alpha)
. This moduli space was considered by Gaitto-Moore-Neitzke in
[
GMN12
,
GMN13
]
.

3.3.
Bonahon-Wong’s quantum trace

Stated skein algebras were designed by Bonahon and Wong in
[
BW11
]
to permit the definition of the quantum trace. Thanks to the deep work of Lê in
[
Le18
]
, the definition of the quantum trace is now very simple. Fix a commutative ring
k
with
A^{1/2}\in k^{\times}
and write
\omega:=A^{-1/2}
its inverse.
First consider the triangle
\mathbb{T}
with edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
and arcs
\alpha_{i}
as in Figure
14
.

Figure 14.
The triangle and some arcs.

For
i\in\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathbf{k}_{i}
be the balanced map sending
e_{i}
to
0
and
e_{i+1},e_{i+2}
to
1
. Using the explicit presentation of the triangle in Theorem
2.31
, it is easy to see
that the linear map
\operatorname{Tr}^{\mathbb{T}}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbb{T})
, sending
(\alpha_{i})_{-+}
to
0
, sending
(\alpha_{i})_{++}
to
Z^{\mathbf{k}_{i}}
and sending
(\alpha_{i})_{--}
to
Z^{-\mathbf{k}_{i}}
, extends to a morphism of algebras. Note that
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{T})
is a
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{T})}
-comodule and that
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbb{T})
is a
k[Z^{\pm 1}]^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{T})}
-comodule. Using the morphism
\varphi:\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\to k[Z^{\pm 1}]
defined by
\varphi(\alpha_{+-})=\varphi(\alpha_{-+})=0
,
\varphi(\alpha_{\varepsilon\varepsilon})=Z^{\varepsilon}
, we see that
\operatorname{tr}^{\mathbb{T}}
is equivariant for the induced
k[Z^{\pm 1}]^{\otimes\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{T})}
-coaction. In particular, for
(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
a triangulated marked surface, we get the commutative diagram

(16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathbb{T}\in F(\Delta)}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\otimes_{e\in\mathring{\mathcal{E}}(\Delta)}\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\right)\otimes\left(\otimes_{\mathbb{T}\in F(\Delta)}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{T})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathbb{T}\in F(\Delta)}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbb{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\otimes_{e\in\mathring{\mathcal{E}}(\Delta)}k[Z_{e}^{\pm 1}]\right)\otimes\left(\otimes_{\mathbb{T}\in F(\Delta)}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbb{T})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathbb{T}}\operatorname{tr}^{\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{L}-\sigma\circ\Delta^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\otimes_{e}\varphi)\otimes(\otimes_{\mathbb{T}}\operatorname{Tr}^{\mathbb{T}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{L}-\sigma\circ\Delta^{R}}

Definition 3.10
.

The
quantum trace
is the unique algebra morphism
\operatorname{Tr}^{\Delta}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
making commuting Diagram
16
.

The following theorem justifies the definition of the reduced stated skein algebra.

Theorem 3.11
.

(Bonahon-Wong
[
BW11
]
for unmarked surfaces, Costantino-Lê
[
CL19
, Theorem
7.12
]
in general)
The quantum trace induces an injective morphism of algebras
\operatorname{Tr}^{\Delta}:\mathcal{S}^{red}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
.

Proof.

For the triangle, it is a straightforward consequence of the presentation of the triangle (Theorem
2.31
) that the quantum trace induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}^{\mathbb{T}}:\mathcal{S}^{red}_{A}(\mathbb{T})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbb{T})
. In general, the injectivity follows from the commutativity of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{red}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathbb{T}\in F(\Delta)}\mathcal{S}^{red}_{A}(\mathbb{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathbb{T}\in F(\Delta)}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbb{T})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathbb{T}}\operatorname{tr}^{\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\Delta}}

∎

The quantum trace preserves the
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma,\mathcal{A};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
grading (
[
Kor19a
, Lemma
2.27
]
).
Also the quantum trace intertwines the Chebyshev-Frobenius morphism with the Frobenius morphism in the sense that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{red}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{red}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ch_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fr_{\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}^{\Delta}}

The commutativity of this diagram follows from the obvious fact that it commutes when
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbb{T}
. So the Chebyshev-Frobenius morphism (for triangulable surfaces) is the restriction of the Frobenius morphism: this is how Bonahon and Wong made it first appear in
[
BW16a
]
.

3.4.
Lê-Yu’s enhancement of the quantum trace

Bonahon and Wong’s quantum trace embeds the reduced stated skein algebras into quantum tori. In order to embed the whole stated skein algebra into some quantum torus, Lê and Yu defined in
[
LYb
]
an enhancement of the quantum trace that we now describe. Let
(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
be a triangulated marked surface and denote by
(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*},\Delta^{*})
the triangulated marked surface obtained from
(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
by gluing a triangle along each boundary arc of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
. So each boundary arc of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
corresponds to two boundary arcs, say
a^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
. Let
i:\mathbf{\Sigma}\to\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}
be the embedding which is the identity outside a small neighborhood of
\mathcal{A}
and embedding
a
into
a^{\prime}
. Note that the morphism
i_{*}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
sends injectivly the basis
\mathcal{B}^{\mathfrak{o}_{+}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
to a subset of the basis
\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathfrak{o}_{+}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
of
\mathcal{S}^{red}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})
(which does not contain any bad arc) so
i_{*}
induces an injective morphism

j:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{A}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}).

We can thus embeds
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
into some quantum torus using the composition:

\phi:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\xrightarrow{j}\mathcal{S}_{A}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{tr}^{\Delta^{*}}}\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*},\Delta^{*}).

The quantum torus
\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*},\Delta^{*})
is very large and the construction can be refined as follows.
Let
\overline{\mathcal{E}}:=\mathcal{E}\bigsqcup\mathcal{A}
. Since a boundary arc
a
is an edge of the triangulation, we denote it by
a
when seen as an element of
\mathcal{E}(\Delta)
and
\widehat{a}
when seen as an element of
\mathcal{A}
. Let
\overline{K}_{\Delta}
denote the set of maps
\mathbf{k}:\overline{\mathcal{E}}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{Z}
such that
(1)
for any face of
\Delta
with edges
a,b,c
, then
\mathbf{k}(a)+\mathbf{k}(b)+\mathbf{k}(c)
is even and
(2)
each
\mathbf{k}(\widehat{a})
is even. Define an injective linear map
i:\overline{K}_{\Delta}\hookrightarrow K_{\Delta^{*}}
sending
\mathbf{k}
to
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}(e)=\mathbf{k}(e)
if
e\in\mathring{\mathcal{E}}(\Delta)
and for
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}(a)=\mathbf{k}(a)+\mathbf{k}(\widehat{a})
,
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}(a^{\prime})=-\mathbf{k}(a)
and
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}(a^{\prime\prime})=0
.
Let
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)\subset\mathcal{Z}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*},\Delta^{*})
be the submodule spanned by elements
Z^{i(\mathbf{k})}
for
\mathbf{k}\in\overline{K}_{\Delta}
. An easy computation shows that the map
\phi
takes values in the smaller quantum torus
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
.

Definition 3.12
.

The injective morphism
\phi:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{Z}}_{\omega}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\Delta)
will be referred to as the
refined quantum trace
.

3.5.
Closed surfaces

It remains to consider closed surfaces. Let
\mathcal{W}_{q}
be the quantum torus generated by
X^{\pm 1},Y^{\pm 1}
with relation
XY=qYX
. Let
\Theta
be the involutive automorphism defined by
\Theta(X)=X^{-1}
and
\Theta(Y)=Y^{-1}
. It decomposes the quantum torus into its eigenspaces
\mathcal{W}_{q}=\mathcal{W}_{q}^{+}\oplus\mathcal{W}_{q}^{-}
where
\mathcal{W}_{q}^{\pm}
is the submodule of elements
z
such that
\Theta(z)=\pm z
.
In genus
1
, one has the

Theorem 3.13
.

(Frohman-Gelca
[
FG00
]
) For
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma_{1,0},\emptyset)
the closed torus, there is an isomorphism
\phi^{FG}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{1,0})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{W}_{q}^{+}
sending the meridian
\lambda\subset\Sigma_{1,0}
to
X+X^{-1}
and the longitude
\mu\subset\Sigma_{1,0}
to
Y+Y^{-1}
.

An important remaining problem is the

Problem 3.14
.

For
g\geq 2
, find an embedding of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,0})
into some quantum torus. If possible, choose one having the same PI-dimension than
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,0})
when
A
is a root of unity.

A candidate for the quantum torus was proposed in
[
KQ19b
]
based on pants decomposition. An important step has been announced by Karuo
[
Kar
]
who will construct a filtration of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma_{g,0})
, based on pants decomposition, and prove that the associated graded algebra can be embedded into some quantum torus.

4.
Relative character varieties

4.1.
Deformation quantization

Let
A_{q}
be an associative unital
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
-algebra which is free and flat as a
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
-module. Consider the
\mathbb{C}
algebra
A_{+1}=A_{q}\otimes_{q=1}\mathbb{C}
and the
\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
algebra
A_{\hbar}=A_{q}\otimes_{q=\exp(\hbar)}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
. We suppose that
A_{+1}
is commutative. Fix
\mathcal{B}
a basis of
A_{q}
, so by flatness,
\mathcal{B}
can be also considered as a basis of
A_{\hbar}
and
A_{+1}
.
The basis
\mathcal{B}
induces an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
-modules
\Psi^{\mathcal{B}}:A_{+1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]\xrightarrow{\cong}A_{\hbar}
sending
b\in\mathcal{B}
to itself. Denote by
\star
the pull-back in
A_{+1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
of the product in
A_{\hbar}
. Define a Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
on
A_{+1}
by the formula:

x\star y-y\star x=\hbar\{x,y\}\pmod{\hbar^{2}}.

Note that the associativity of
\star
implies the Jacobi identity for
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
.
We say that the algebra
(A_{+1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]],\star)
is a
deformation quantization
of the Poisson algebra
(A_{+1},\{\cdot,\cdot\})
and refer to (
[
Kon03
]
,
[
GRS05
]

II.2
) for details on the subject. If
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is another basis, then
\Psi^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\circ\Psi^{\mathcal{B}}
is an algebra isomorphism
(A_{+1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]],\star_{\mathcal{B}})\cong(A_{+1}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]],\star_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}})
whose reduction modulo
\hbar
is the identity: such an isomorphism is called a
gauge equivalence
and it is clear that two gauge equivalent star products induces the same Poisson bracket, in particular
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is independent on the choice of
\mathcal{B}
. Note that when
A_{+1}
is reduced and finitely generated,
\mathrm{Specm}(A_{+1})
is a Poisson variety. For instance, for
\mathbb{E}
a quadratic pair, the quantum torus
\mathbb{T}_{\hbar}(\mathbb{E})
where
q=\exp(\hbar)
, is a deformation quantization of the
Poisson torus
\mathcal{\chi}(\mathbb{E})
. Indeed, from the equality
Z_{a+b}=q^{-(a,b)}Z_{a}Z_{b}=q^{+(a,b)}Z_{b}Z_{a}
and setting
q=\exp{\hbar}
, we get that

Z_{a}\star Z_{b}-Z_{b}\star Z_{a}=(q^{(a,b)}-q^{-(a,b)})Z_{a+b}=\hbar 2(a,b)Z_{a+b}\pmod{\hbar^{2}},

giving the Poisson bracket
\{Z_{a},Z_{b}\}=2(a,b)Z_{a+b}
of
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{E})
.

Remark 4.1
.

If
\Psi_{A}:A^{1}_{q}\to A^{2}_{q}
is an algebra morphism, it induces some morphisms
\Psi_{\hbar}:A^{1}_{\hbar}\to A^{2}_{\hbar}
and
\Psi_{+1}:A^{1}_{+1}\to A^{2}_{+1}
. Since
\Psi_{+1}
is the reduction modulo
\hbar
of
\Psi_{\hbar}
, it follows from the definition of the Poisson bracket that
\Psi_{+1}
is a Poisson morphism.

Definition 4.2
.

For
\mathbf{\Sigma}
a marked surface, we denote by
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}):=\mathrm{Specm}(\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma}))
and
\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma}):=\mathrm{Specm}(\mathcal{S}^{red}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma}))
the Poisson varieties associated to
A^{1/2}:=\exp(\hbar/4)
(so that
q=\exp(\hbar)
).

Note that by Remark
4.1
, the gluing maps
\theta_{a\#b}
and the comodule maps
\Delta^{L/R}_{a}
are Poisson.

Notations 4.3
.

When considering an affine complex variety
X
, we will denote by
\mathcal{O}[X]
its algebra of regular functions (so
X=\mathrm{Specm}(\mathcal{O}[X])
). A closed point
x\in X
is a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{x}\subset\mathbb{C}[X]
which is the kernel of a (unique) character
\chi_{x}:\mathcal{O}[X]\to\mathbb{C}
. Even though
x
and
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
are the same object, we use three letters
x,\mathrm{m}_{x},\chi_{x}
to denote it.

In view of classifying the weight representations of stated algebras, we will be interested in computing the symplectic leaves of these Poisson varieties. Let
X
be an affine complex Poisson variety. Define a first partition
X=X^{0}\bigsqcup\ldots\bigsqcup X^{n}
where
X^{0}
is the smooth locus of
X
and for
i=0,\ldots,n-1
,
X^{i+1}
is the smooth locus of
X\setminus X^{i}
. Then each
X^{i}
is a smooth complex affine variety that can be seen as an analytic Poisson variety. Define an equivalence relation
\sim
on
X^{i}
by
writing
x\sim y
if there exists a finite sequence
x=p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}=y
and functions
h_{0},\ldots,h_{k-1}\in\mathcal{O}[X^{i}]
such that
p_{i+1}
is obtained from
p_{i}
by deriving along the Hamiltonian flow of
h_{i}
. Write
X^{i}=\bigsqcup_{j}X^{i,j}
the orbits of this relation. Note that the
X^{i,j}
are analytic subvarieties: they are the biggest connected smooth symplectic subvarieties of
X^{i}
.

Definition 4.4
.

The elements
X^{i,j}
of the (analytic) partition
X=\bigsqcup_{i,j}X^{i,j}
are called the
symplectic leaves
of
X
.

Recall the coaction map
\Delta^{L}:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to(\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}])^{\otimes\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
coming from gluing bigons to the boundary arcs. It induces an algebraic group action
\nabla^{L}:(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
which is Poisson when
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
has the Poisson structure of
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{B})
(see bellow). Using the diagonal inclusion
\mathbb{C}^{*}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
sending
z
to
\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&z^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
, we get an algebraic (left) group action
\nabla:(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. This action restricts to a group action
\nabla^{red}:(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. Define an equivalence relation
\sim
on these varieties by setting
x\sim y
if there exists
g\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{\mathcal{A}}
such that
g\cdot x
and
y
belong to the same symplectic leaf.

Definition 4.5
.

The
equivariant symplectic leaves
of
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and
\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
are the equivalence classes for this relation.

As we shall see in Section
5.5
, the problem of classifying the weight representations of stated skein algebras is deeply related to the following:

Problem 4.6
.

Compute the equivariant symplectic leaves of
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
and
\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

As we shall review, the problem was solved:

(1)

for closed surfaces by Goldman
[
Gol84
]
;

(2)

for unmarked non-closed surfaces, independently by Fock-Rosly
[
FR99
]
and Guruprasad-Huebschmann-Jeffrey-Weitsman
[
GHJW97
]
;

(3)

for the once-punctured monogon
\mathbf{m}_{1}
and
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{+}:=\underline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{0,2}
by Alekseev-Malkin
[
AM94
]
;

(4)

for the bigon, independently by Alekseev-Malkin
[
AM94
]
and Hodges-Levasseur
[
Hod93
]
;

(5)

Ganev-Jordan-Safranov
[
GJSa
]
found an explicit open dense symplectic leaf in
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,0}^{0})
, for
g\geq 1
.

Let us first state a trivial, but useful result towards the resolution of this problem. Consider an algebra
A_{q}
as before and let
x\in\mathcal{A}_{q}
be such that
xA_{q}=A_{q}x
, i.e. the left and right (and bilateral) ideals generated by
x
coincide. Let
I_{q}=(x)\subset A_{q}
this ideal and
I=I_{q}\otimes_{q=1}\mathbb{C}\subset A_{+1}
. Since we have
[I_{q},A_{q}]\subset I_{q}
, it follows from the definition of the Poisson bracket that
\{I,A_{+1}\}\subset I
, i.e. that
I
is a Poisson ideal of
A_{+1}
. Partition the set
X=\mathrm{Specm}(A_{+1})
into
X=X^{0}\bigsqcup X^{1}
where
X^{0}
is the open subset of
x\in X
such that
\chi_{x}(I)=0
and
X^{1}
its closed complement. Clearly each set
X^{i}
is a disjoint union of symplectic leaves, i.e. the partition into symplectic leaves is a refinement of the partition
X=X^{0}\bigsqcup X^{1}
.

An easy skein manipulation shows the following:

Lemma 4.7
.

(Lê-Yu
[
LYb
, Lemma
4.4.(a)
]
) Let
p\in\mathcal{P}^{\partial}
be a boundary puncture and
\alpha(p)_{-+}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
its associated bad arc. For any
[D,s]\in\mathcal{B}^{\mathfrak{o}^{+}}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\alpha(p)_{-+}[D,s]=A^{n/2}[D,s]\alpha(p)_{-+}
. In particular
\alpha(p)_{-+}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\alpha(p)_{-+}
.

For
\mathbf{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{P}^{\partial}\to\{0,1\}
, denote by
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathbf{\varepsilon})}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\subset\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
the subset of these
x\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
such that
\chi_{x}(\alpha(p)_{-+})=0
if
\mathbf{\varepsilon}(p)=0
and
\chi_{x}(\alpha(p)_{-+})\neq 0
else.

Definition 4.8
.

We call the
bad arcs partition
the partition
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{X}^{(\mathbf{\varepsilon})}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Note that for
\mathbf{\varepsilon}=0
(the map sending every
p
to
0
), one has
\mathcal{X}^{(0)}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
by definition.
By Lemma
4.7
and the preceding discussion, the partition into symplectic leaves is a refinement of the bad arc partition. Moreover, it is clear from the definition of the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{\mathcal{A}}
action that each set
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathbf{\varepsilon})}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is preserved by
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{\mathcal{A}}
, so we have our first tool towards the resolution of Problem
4.6
:

Lemma 4.9
.

The partition into equivariant symplectic leaves is a refinement of the bad arcs partition.

Let us state a second obvious remark towards the resolution of Problem
4.6
. Recall from Definition
3.5
, that for each inner puncture
p
we defined a central element
\gamma_{p}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}(\Sigma)
and for each boundary component
\partial
, we defined an invertible central element
\alpha_{\partial}\in\mathcal{S}_{A}^{red}(\Sigma)
. Let
\mathrm{Cas}\subset\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
(resp.
\mathrm{Cas}^{red}\subset\mathcal{S}_{+1}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
) denote the subgroup generated by the elements
\gamma_{p}
(resp. by the elements
\gamma_{p},\alpha_{\partial}^{\pm 1}
). Since these elements are central in the skein algebras with parameter
A=\exp(\hbar/2)
, the elements in
\mathrm{Cas}
and
\mathrm{Cas}^{red}
are Casimir elements, i.e. they are in the kernel of the Poisson bracket. Therefore, if we consider the following
Casimir partition

\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\bigsqcup_{\pi:\mathrm{Cas}\to\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{X}_{(\pi)}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\quad\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\bigsqcup_{\pi:\mathrm{Cas}^{red}\to\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{X}^{red}_{(\pi)}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),

where the
\pi
are characters over the Casimir groups and
\mathcal{X}_{(\pi)}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the (algebraic) subset of elements
x
such that
\chi_{x}(c)=\pi(c)
, for all
c\in\mathrm{Cas}
and similarly for the reduced version, then

Lemma 4.10
.

The partition into symplectic leaves is a refinement of the Casimir partition.

Note that the group
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{\mathcal{A}}
preserves the Casimir leaves
\mathcal{X}_{(\pi)}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
but not the leaves
\mathcal{X}^{red}_{(\pi)}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Let us make a final remark:

Lemma 4.11
.

If
\mathcal{S}_{A}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is commutative for
A
generic, then for every
x\in\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\mathcal{X}^{(0)}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
, then the singleton
\{x\}
is a symplectic leaf of
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}^{bad}\subset\mathcal{S}_{A}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
be the ideal generated by the bad arcs. If
\mathcal{S}_{A}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is commutative for
A=\exp(\hbar)
, then we have
[\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]\subset\mathcal{I}^{bad}
, so by definition of the Poisson bracket we have
\{\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\}\subset\mathcal{I}^{bad}
. Therefore the restriction of the Poisson bracket to
\mathcal{X}^{red}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
vanishes.
∎

4.2.
First examples and the work of Semenov-Tian-Shansky

Let us study the cases of the bigon
\mathbb{B}
, the once-punctured bigon
\mathbb{D}_{1}
and the once-punctured monogon
\mathbf{m}_{1}
for which Problem
4.6
has been solved by Alekseev-Malkin
[
AM94
]
and Hodges-Levasseur
[
Hod93
]
.
At this stage, it is useful to understand the structure of the quantum
R
-matrix
\mathscr{R}
. Consider the matrices in
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
:

E=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad F=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad H=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Consider these matrices as operators acting on a
2
-dimensional vector space
V
with ordered basis
(v_{+},v_{-})
(the standard representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
). Define an endomorphism
q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}\in\operatorname{End}(V\otimes V)
by
q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}\cdot v_{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes v_{\varepsilon_{2}}:=A^{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}v_{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes v_{\varepsilon_{2}}
. Then
\mathscr{R}
is the matrix in the ordered basis
(v_{+}\otimes v_{+},v_{+}\otimes v_{-},v_{-}\otimes v_{+},v_{-}\otimes v_{-})
of the operator

\mathscr{R}=\tau\circ q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}\exp_{q}\left((q-q^{-1})E\otimes F\right)=\tau\circ q^{\frac{H\otimes H}{2}}\circ\left(\mathds{1}_{2}+(q-q^{-1})E\otimes F\right).

Define the classical r-matrices:

r^{+}:=\frac{1}{2}H\otimes H+2E\otimes F,\quad r^{-}:=\frac{1}{2}H\otimes H+2F\otimes E.

Then, writing
A^{1/2}=\exp(\hbar/2)
, one has

(17)

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}\equiv\tau(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}+\hbar r^{+})\pmod{\hbar^{2}}\equiv(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}+\hbar r^{-})\tau\pmod{\hbar^{2}},

(18)

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}^{-1}\equiv(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}-\hbar r^{+})\tau\pmod{\hbar^{2}}\equiv\tau(\mathds{1}\otimes\mathds{1}-\hbar r^{-})\pmod{\hbar^{2}},

The bigon:
Let
\alpha\subset\mathbb{B}
be an arc linking
a_{L}
and
a_{R}
and
\alpha_{ij}
the stated arc with state
i
on
a_{L}
and
j
on
a_{R}
. By Theorem
2.31
,
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{B})=\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
is generated by the elements
\alpha_{ij},i,j=\pm
together with the relations, written in matrix form using
N:=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{++}&\alpha_{+-}\\
\alpha_{-+}&\alpha_{--}\end{pmatrix}
, given by
\det_{q}(N)=1
and the so-called Faddeev-Reshetikhin-Takhtadjian relation

N\odot N=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(N\odot N)\mathscr{R}.

So we get Equation (
7
) by identifying the matrix coefficients. Replacing
A
by
\exp(\hbar/2)
and developing using Equations (
17
) (
18
), we find

\displaystyle N\odot N\equiv(1-\hbar r^{+})\tau(N\odot N)\tau(1+\hbar r^{+})\pmod{\hbar^{2}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\tau(N\odot N)\tau-N\odot N\equiv\hbar\left(r^{+}(N\odot N)-(N\odot N)r^{+}\right)\pmod{\hbar^{2}}

Therefore
\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{B})
can be identified with the variety
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
together with the Poisson bracket defined by

(19)

\{N\otimes N\}^{D}=r^{+}(N\odot N)-(N\odot N)r^{+}.

For further use, we define
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[a,b,c,d]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(ad-bd-1)$}\right.}
and the isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbb{B})\cong\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
identifies
\alpha_{++},\alpha_{+-},\alpha_{-+},\alpha_{--}
with
a,b,c,d
respectively.
Since the coproduct is defined as a gluing map, it is Poisson. We will denote by
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
the obtained Poisson-Lie group (the D stands for Drinfel’d who first defined it in
[
Dri83
]
). We can rewrite Equation (
19
) as

\displaystyle\{a,b\}^{D}

\displaystyle=-ab

\displaystyle\{a,c\}^{D}

\displaystyle=-ac

\displaystyle\{b,c\}^{D}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\{d,b\}^{D}

\displaystyle=db

\displaystyle\{d,c\}^{D}

\displaystyle=dc

\displaystyle\{a,d\}^{D}

\displaystyle=-2bc

In this case, the bad arcs partition writes

\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}=X_{00}\bigsqcup X_{01}\bigsqcup X_{10}\bigsqcup X_{11},

where
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
is in
X_{11}
if
bd\neq 0
, is in
X_{10}
if
b=0,c\neq 0
, is in
X_{01}
if
c=0,b\neq 0
and is in
X_{00}
if it is diagonal. The Weil group of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is
W=\{w_{0},w_{1}\}
where
w_{0}
is the class of the identity
\dot{w}_{0}:=\mathds{1}_{2}
and
w_{1}
is the class of
\dot{w}_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
. Denote by
B^{+}
(resp.
B^{-}
) the subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
of upper (resp. lower) triangular matrices. A simple computation shows that

X_{ij}=B^{+}\dot{w}_{i}B^{+}\cap B^{-}\dot{w}_{j}B^{-}.

In other terms, the bad arc partition of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
coincides with the double Bruhat cells decomposition of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
. As we shall review, Hodges and Levasseur have proved that in this case, the bad arc partition and the equivariant symplectic leaves partition coincide, i.e. the double Bruhat cells are the equivariant symplectic leaves.

The once-punctured monogon:

Write
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]:=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{m}_{1})
. This algebra is also a deformation of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
but with a different Poisson structure, that we will denote by
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
by reference to Semenov-Tian-Shansky who first defined it in
[
STS85
]
. This Poisson structure is no longer compatible with the coproduct, i.e.
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
is not a Poisson Lie group. Let
\alpha\subset\mathbf{m}_{1}
be the unique corner arc with endpoint
v
and
w
such that
v<_{\mathfrak{o}_{+}}w
and
\alpha_{ij}
the stated arc with state
i
on
v
and
j
on
w
and
h(v)<h(w)
(in the terminology of Section
2.8
,
\alpha
is of type
d
).

This time, in order to identify
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{m}_{1})
with
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
, we set

N=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}:=C^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{++}&\alpha_{+-}\\
\alpha_{-+}&\alpha_{--}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\alpha_{-+}&-\alpha_{--}\\
\alpha_{++}&\alpha_{+-}\end{pmatrix}.

Theorem
2.31
shows that
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]
is generated by
a,b,c,d
with relations
\det_{q^{2}}(N)=1
and

(\mathds{1}_{1}\odot N)\mathscr{R}^{-1}(\mathds{1}_{1}\odot N)\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{R}(\mathds{1}_{1}\odot N)\mathscr{R}^{-1}(\mathds{1}_{1}\odot N)

.
Replacing
A
by
\exp(\hbar/2)
and developing using Equations (
17
) (
18
) as before, we find

\{N\otimes N\}^{STS}=-(\mathds{1}_{1}\odot N)r^{+}(N\odot\mathds{1}_{2})+\tau(N\odot N)\tau r^{+}-r^{-}(N\odot N)+(N\odot\mathds{1}_{2})r^{-}(\mathds{1}_{2}\odot N).

We can develop these equations to find

\displaystyle[\alpha_{++},\alpha_{+-}]=(q^{-1}-q)\alpha_{-+}\alpha_{++}

\displaystyle[\alpha_{+-},\alpha_{--}]=(q^{-1}-q)\alpha_{--}\alpha_{-+}

\displaystyle\alpha_{--}\alpha_{-+}=q^{2}\alpha_{-+}\alpha_{--}

\displaystyle\alpha_{-+}\alpha_{++}=q^{2}\alpha_{++}\alpha_{-+}

\displaystyle[\alpha_{+-},\alpha_{-+}]=0

\displaystyle[\alpha_{++},\alpha_{--}]=(q^{-1}-q)\alpha_{-+}^{2}+(1-q^{2})\alpha_{+-}\alpha_{-+}

\displaystyle\alpha_{--}\alpha_{++}-q^{2}\alpha_{+-}\alpha_{-+}=A

From which we get

\displaystyle\{c,d\}^{STS}

\displaystyle=2ac

\displaystyle\{d,b\}^{STS}

\displaystyle=2ab

\displaystyle\{d,a\}^{STS}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\{b,a\}^{STS}

\displaystyle=2ab

\displaystyle\{a,c\}^{STS}

\displaystyle=2ac

\displaystyle\{c,b\}^{STS}

\displaystyle=2a(a-d).

The bad arcs partition writes

\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}\bigsqcup\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1},

where
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}=B^{-}B^{+}
is the subset of matrices
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
with
a\neq 0
and
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}=B^{-}CB^{+}
is the set of those matrices for which
a=0
. So the bad arcs partition coincides with the simple Bruhat cells decomposition associated to the Cartan decomposition
(B^{-},B^{+})
.

Note that Lemmas
4.11
and
4.10
imply that

(1)

For
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
, the singleton
\{g\}
is a symplectic leaf of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
and

(2)

the symplectic leaves of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}
are included in the algebraic sets
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}(c):=\{g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}|tr(g)=c\}
for
c\in\mathbb{C}
(since
a+d
is Casimir).

As we shall review, it follows from the work of Alekseev-Malkin in
[
AM94
]
that the symplectic leaves in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}
are the analytic sets
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}\cap C
, for
C
a conjugacy class. The algebraic sets
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}(c)
will be called symplectic cores of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
in Section
5.5
. Note that
\mathbf{m}_{1}
has exactly one boundary arc and the induced Poisson action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
is the action by conjugaison. So, writing
\varphi(z):=\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&z^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
, the sets
\{\varphi(z)g\varphi(z^{-1}),z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\}
for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
are equivariant symplectic leaves.

Note also that the identification
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\cong\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{m}_{1})
that we made contains an arbitrary choice: we could have precompose this identification by the Cartan involution, i.e. have stated
N:=M(\alpha)C^{-1}
instead of
N:=C^{-1}M(\alpha)
. With this new identification, the bad arcs decomposition would have written

{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\prime}}^{STS}={}^{0}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\bigsqcup{}^{1}\operatorname{SL}_{2},

where
{}^{0}\operatorname{SL}_{2}=B^{+}B^{-}
is the set of these
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}
with
d\neq 0
and
{}^{1}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is its complementary.

Remark 4.12
.

The algebra
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]
appeared in the work of Lyubashenko in
[
Lyu95
]
who revisited Shan’s Tannakian theorem in
[
Maj95
]
. In short, Shan’s theorem says that for a Tannakian ribbon category, i.e. a ribbon category
\mathcal{C}
with a tensor faithful functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Vect}
, where
\operatorname{Vect}
is the category of finite dimensional vector spaces, then there exists a coribbon Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}
and a ribbon equivalence of categories
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{H}-\mathrm{Comod}
.
Following the classical work of Grothendieck-Deligne, Shan defined
\mathcal{H}=\mathrm{Nat}(F)
has a space of natural transformations of
F
. Lyubashenko found a much more explicit definition for
\mathcal{H}
. He considers
\mathcal{F}:=\int^{V\in\mathcal{C}}V^{*}\otimes V
and turns
\mathcal{F}
into an algebra object using the product defined as follows: for
V,W\in\mathcal{C}
then
\mu:[V\otimes V^{*}]\otimes[W\otimes W^{*}]\to[V\otimes W]\otimes[W^{*}\otimes V^{*}]
is the morphism in
\mathcal{C}
given by the image by the Reshetikhin-Turaev functor of

\mu:=F^{RT}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{product_Lyubashenko.eps}}}}}}}$}\right).

Then
\mathcal{H}=F(\mathcal{F})\in\operatorname{Vect}
is the desired cobraided Hopf algebra with coproduct
\Delta([v^{*}\otimes v]):=\sum_{i}[v^{*}\otimes v_{i}]\otimes[v_{i}^{*}\otimes v]
, for
\{v_{i}\}_{i}
a basis of
V
, co-unit
\epsilon([v^{*}\otimes v])=ev_{V}(v^{*}\otimes v)
and co-R-matrix
\mathbf{r}([v^{*}\otimes v]\otimes[w^{*}\otimes w]):=\epsilon([(v^{*}\otimes w^{*})\otimes c_{V,W}(v,w)])
.
As noticed by Gunningham-Jordan-Safranov in
[
GJSb
]
, when
\mathcal{C}=U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}-\mathrm{Mod}
with
q
generic, and
F
is the forgetful functor, the Lyubashenko algebra
\mathcal{H}
is the stated skein algebra of the once-punctured monogon. So Lyubashenko’s Tannakian theorem states that the category of
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules (
q
is generic here) is equivalent to the category of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]
-comodules. This is where the literature becomes confusing: since
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
is the restricted dual of
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, the category of
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules is equivalent to the category of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
-comodules as well ! Worst: Lyubashenko named
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]
the
quantum function algebra
(hence the letter
\mathcal{F}
), so with the same name that
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
. Because of this confusion, many authors denote by the same symbol the algebras
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
and
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]
though, as we just saw, they are not isomorphic as algebras and deform two very different Poisson structures. Since
\mathbf{m}_{1}
is obtained from
\mathbb{B}
by fusioning the two boundary arcs, the relation between the two algebras is clear:
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]
is the fusion (in the sense of Definition
2.19
) of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
.

The Heisenberg double and its twist

We now consider the once-punctured bigon
\mathbb{D}_{1}
and the marked surface
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{+}:=\underline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{0,2}
, so both are annuli with two boundary arcs, and both boundary arcs are in the same boundary component in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
whereas the two boundary arcs in
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{+}
are in two distinct boundary components. Write
D_{q}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}):=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
and
D_{q}^{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}):=\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{1}^{+})
. Both their fundamental groupoids admit presentations with two generators
\alpha,\beta
, drawn in Figure
15
, and no relation.

Figure 15.
The marked surfaces
\mathbb{D}_{1}
and
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{+}
defining the Heisenberg doubles
D_{q}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
and
D_{q}^{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
.

Consider the matrices
N(\alpha)=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{++}&\alpha_{+-}\\
\alpha_{-+}&\alpha_{--}\end{pmatrix},N(\beta)=\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{++}&\beta_{+-}\\
\beta_{-+}&\beta_{--}\end{pmatrix}
. Theorem
2.31
shows that
D_{q}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
is generated by the elements
\alpha_{ij},\beta_{kl}
with relations
\det_{q}(N(\alpha))=\det_{q}(N(\beta))=1
and

N(\alpha)\odot N(\alpha)=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(N(\alpha)\odot N(\alpha))\mathscr{R},N(\beta)\odot N(\beta)=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(N(\beta)\odot N(\beta))\mathscr{R},N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta)=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta))\mathscr{R},

therefore the Poisson structure is described by

\{N(a)\otimes N(b)\}^{D}=r^{+}(N(a)\odot N(b))-(N(a)\odot N(b))r^{+}\quad\mbox{, for }(a,b)=(\alpha,\alpha),(\beta,\beta),(\alpha,\beta).

The algebra
D_{q}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
has a structure of Hopf algebra that we now describe. Let
i:\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{D}_{2}
be the embedding which is the identity on the boundary and embeds the two inner punctures of
\mathbb{D}_{2}
inside the inner puncture of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
. Note that
\mathbb{D}_{2}
is obtained from
\mathbb{D}_{1}\cup\mathbb{D}_{1}
by gluing two boundary arcs, so we get a gluing map
\theta:\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{2})\hookrightarrow D_{q}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\otimes 2}
. The
Bigelow coproduct
is then defined as the composition

\Delta:D_{q}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})\xrightarrow{i_{*}}\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbb{D}_{2})\xrightarrow{\theta}D_{q}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\otimes 2}.

Figure 16.
An illustration of Bigelow’s coproduct.

It appeared in the work of Bigelow in a different language in
[
Big14
]
. In particular,
D(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
received a structure of Poisson-Lie group from this coproduct and was called the
Heisenberg double
by Drinfel’d. Note that the reduced skein algebra
\mathcal{S}_{A}^{red}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
is also a Hopf algebra with this structure and it is proved in
[
Kor19a
, Theorem1]
that it is isomorphic to the simply laced quantum group
\widetilde{U}_{q}\mathfrak{gl}_{2}
.

In
D_{q}^{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
, the relations read

N(\alpha)\odot N(\alpha)=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(N(\alpha)\odot N(\alpha))\mathscr{R},N(\beta)\odot N(\beta)=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(N(\beta)\odot N(\beta))\mathscr{R},N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta)=\mathscr{R}(N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta))\mathscr{R}

so
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}):=\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{D}_{1}^{+})
has a Poisson bracket described by

\{N(\alpha)\otimes N(\beta)\}^{+}=r_{+}(N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta))+(N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta))r_{+},\quad\{N(a)\otimes N(a)\}^{+}=r^{+}(N(a)\odot N(a))-(N(a)\odot N(a))r^{+},

for
a=\alpha,\beta
.
The Poisson variety
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
was studied by Alekseev-Malkin in
[
AM94
]
inspired by the work of Semenov-Tian-Shansky. More precisely, they consider the bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{AM}:=-\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{+}
for which, using the notations
r:=r^{+}
and
r^{*}:=-r^{-}
, one has (compare with
[
AM94
, Equation
(80
)]
):

\{N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta)\}^{AM}=-\left(r(N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta))+(N(\alpha)\odot N(\beta))r^{*}\right).

For
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
, the bad arcs partition writes

D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})=D_{00}\bigsqcup D_{01}\bigsqcup D_{10}\bigsqcup D_{11},

where

D_{ij}=\{(g_{1},g_{2})|g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{i},g_{2}g_{1}^{-1}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{j}\}.

Note that, by comparing the previous formulas for the Poisson brackets, it is clear that the diagonal embedding
\delta:\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}\to D(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
sending
g
to
(g,g)
is Poisson. Denote by
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\delta}\subset D(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
its image. Introduce also the subalgebra

\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}:=\left\{\left(\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&x\\
0&\lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{-1}&0\\
y&\lambda\end{pmatrix}\right),\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*},x,y\in\mathbb{C}\right\}\subset D(\operatorname{SL}_{2}).

The bad arcs leaves admit the following characterisation, which appear in
[
AM94
]
:

D_{ij}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\delta}(\dot{w}_{i},\mathds{1}_{2})\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}\cap\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}(\mathds{1}_{2},\dot{w}_{j})\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\delta}.

Theorem 4.13
.

(Alekseev-Malkin
[
AM94
, Theorem
2
]
) The bad arcs leaves
D_{ij}
are symplectic in
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
.

Let
\mathfrak{sl}_{2},\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{*},D(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
denote the Lie algebras of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}\cong\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\delta},\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}
and
D(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
respectively and note that
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{*}=D(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. The triple
(\mathfrak{sl}_{2},\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{*},D(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}))
is called a
Manin triple

and it encodes (and is determined by) at the infinitesimal level the Poisson Lie group structure of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}

(see
[
CP95
]
for a detailed account on the subject). Note that the
(2:1)
map
\varphi:\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}\to(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0})^{STS}
, sending
(g_{1},g_{2})
to
g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}
is Poisson: it corresponds to the embedding of marked surfaces drawn in Figure
17
.

Figure 17.
The embedding
\mathbf{m}_{1}\to\mathbb{D}^{+}_{1}
defining the map
\varphi:D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})\to(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0})^{STS}
.

The work of Semenov-Tian-Shansky and its generalizations:

A useful tool to compute the symplectic leaves of a Poisson variety is the notion of dual pairs introduced by Weinstein in
[
Wei83
]
. For
X
a smooth Poisson variety and
A\subset\mathcal{O}(X)
a subspace, define

A^{\perp}:=\{f\in\mathcal{O}(X)|\{f,a\}=0,\forall a\in A\}.

Definition 4.14
.

Let
X,Y,Z
three smooth Poisson manifolds such that
Z
is symplectic. A
dual pair
is a pair
(f,g)
of Poisson morphisms
f:Z\to X,g:Z\to Y
which are surjective submersions and such that

f^{*}(\mathcal{O}(X))^{\perp}=g^{*}(\mathcal{O}(Y))\quad\mbox{and}\quad g^{*}(\mathcal{O}(Y))^{\perp}=f^{*}(\mathcal{O}(X))^{\perp}.

Theorem 4.15
.

(Weinstein
[
Wei83
, Section
8
]
) For
(f,g)
a dual pair, then the symplectic leaves of
X
are the connected components of the
f(g^{-1}(y))
for
y\in Y
and the symplectic leaves of
Y
are the connected components of the
g(f^{-1}(x))
for
x\in X
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{*},D(\mathfrak{g}))
be a Manin triple of finite dimensional Lie bialgebras and let
G,G^{*},D(G)
denote the connected, simply connected Poisson Lie groups obtained by exponenting the three bialgebras. The equality maps
\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*}=D(\mathfrak{g})
and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{g}=D(\mathfrak{g})
induce some Poisson maps
\Psi_{1}:G\times G^{*}\to D(G)
and
\Psi_{2}:G^{*}\times G\to D(G)
which are local diffeomorphisms in the neighbourhoods of the identities. The Poisson Lie group is called
factorizable
if
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are global diffeomorphisms. In this case, consider the diffeomorphism
\Psi_{2}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{1}:G\times G^{*}\cong G^{*}\times G
sending
(g,g^{*})
to
\Psi_{2}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{1}(g,g^{*})=(g^{g^{*}},g\cdot g^{*})
. The formulas
(g,g^{*})\mapsto g^{g^{*}}
and
(g,g^{*})\mapsto g\cdot g^{*}
define a right action of
G^{*}
on
G
and a left action of
G
on
G^{*}
respectively, which are called
dressing actions
. In the factorizable case, Semonov-Tian-Shansky proved in
[
STS85
, Proposition
14
]
that
D(G)
is symplectic and that in the correspondance

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\cong{}_{G^{*}}\backslash^{D(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$D(G)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$G^{*}$}\right.}\cong G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

the pair
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is a dual pair. Using Theorem
4.15
, Semenov-Tian-Shansky concludes in
[
STS85
, Proposition
15
]
that the symplectic leaves of
G
are the connected components of the
G^{*}
dressing orbits
\pi_{1}(\pi_{2}^{-1}(g)),g\in G
with a similar result by exchanging
G
and
G^{*}
.

Unfortunately, the Poisson Lie group
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
is not factorizable: the product map
\Psi_{1}:\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\delta}\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}\to D(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
is
2:1
and is not surjective.
Moreover, by Lemma
4.10
,
D(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
is very far from been symplectic: it has an infinite number of symplectic leaves. The idea of Alekseev-Malkin is to replace
D(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
by the symplectic leaves
D_{ij}^{+}
of
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
. The inclusion maps
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\delta},\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}\hookrightarrow D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
are also Poisson. Let

\Gamma:=\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\delta}\cap\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}=\{(\pm\mathds{1}_{2},\pm\mathds{1}_{2})\}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

and write
\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{D}={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\delta}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Gamma$}\right.}
and
\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}}={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Gamma$}\right.}
. Recall the definition of the simple Bruhat cells
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{i}
and
{}^{i}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
corresponding to the Cartan data
(B_{+},B_{-})
and
(B_{-},B_{+})
respectively. Alekseev and Malkin proved that in the correspondance

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{ij}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{i}\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{D}\cong{}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}\cap D_{ij}}\backslash{D_{ij}^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$D_{ij}^{+}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}\cap D_{ij}$}\right.}\cong{\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{j}}^{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

the maps
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
form a dual pair, so Weinstein’s Theorem
4.15
implies that the symplectic leaves of
\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{D}
are the intersections of the
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}
dressing orbits with the Bruhat cells
{\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{j}}^{D}
. Since the quotient map
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}\to\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{D}
is a regular Poisson covering (it is étale), the symplectic leaves of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
are the pull-backs by this map of the symplectic leaves of
\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{D}
, and we get the

Theorem 4.16
.

(Hodges-Levasseur
[
Hod93
, Theorem
B.2.1
]
, Alekseev-Malkin
[
AM94
, Section
4
]
) The symplectic leaves of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
are

(1)

The leaves
\mathfrak{F}_{11}(\lambda):=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&\lambda b\\
b&d\end{pmatrix}|b\neq 0,ad-\lambda b^{2}=1\right\},\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
;

(2)

the leaves
X_{01}
and
X_{10}
;

(3)

the singletons
\{g\}
for
g\in X_{11}
.

In particular, the equivariant symplectic leaves of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
are the double Bruhat cells
X_{ij}
.

Similarly, by exchanging the roles of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}
, we obtain that the symplectic leaves of
\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}}
and, using the Poisson isomorphism
\varphi:\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}}^{STS}
, sending
(g_{+},g_{-})
to
g_{-}^{-1}g_{+}
, and noting that the dressing action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
on
\overline{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{*}}
corresponds through
\varphi
to the action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
on
{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}}^{STS}
by conjugacy,
we see that the leaves
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}\cap C
, for
C
a conjugacy class, are symplectic in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
. Together with Lemma
4.11
, we get the

Theorem 4.17
.

(Alekseev-Malkin
[
AM94
, Section
4
]
)
The symplectic leaves of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
are

(1)

The leaves
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}\cap C
, for
C
a conjugacy class;

(2)

the singletons
\{g\}
for
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{1}
.

4.3.
The classical fusion operation

We now describe the classical equivalent of Theorem
2.20
. Let
\mathbf{r}_{\hbar}:\mathcal{O}_{\hbar}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
be the co-R-matrix for parameter
q:=\exp(\hbar)
and note that

\mathbf{r}_{\hbar}\equiv\epsilon\otimes\epsilon+\hbar r^{+}\pmod{\hbar^{2}}.

In this formula, we see
r^{+}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\otimes\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
as an element of the Zariski tangent space of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}
at the neutral element
(\mathds{1}_{2},\mathds{1}_{2})
, i.e.
r^{+}\in\mathrm{Der}(\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]^{\otimes 2},\mathbb{C}_{\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon})
is a derivation valued in
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]^{\otimes 2}
-module structure induced by
\epsilon\otimes\epsilon
.

Let
G
be an affine algebraic Poisson Lie group with Poisson structure given by a classical r-matrix
r^{+}\in\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
(i.e. with Poisson structure given by the cocomutator
\delta:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
,
\delta(X)=[X,r^{+}]
). A
G
-Poisson affine variety is a complex affine variety
X
with an algebraic Poisson action
G\times X\to X
.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
X
be a
G^{2}
-Poisson affine variety and denote by
\Delta_{G\times G}:\mathcal{O}[X]\to\mathcal{O}[G]\otimes\mathcal{O}[X]
it comodule map. Wite
\Delta^{1}:=(id\otimes\epsilon\times id)\circ\Delta_{G\times G}
and
\Delta^{2}:=(\epsilon\otimes id\otimes id)
.
The
fusion
of
X
is the
G
-Poisson affine variety
X^{\circledast}
define by

(1)

As a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra,
\mathcal{O}[X^{\circledast}]=\mathcal{O}[X]
.

(2)

For
x\in\mathcal{O}[X]
and
i=1,2
, write
\Delta^{i}(x)=\sum x_{(i)}^{\prime}\otimes x_{(i)}^{\prime\prime}
. The Poisson bracket is defined by

\{x,y\}^{\circledast}:=\{x,y\}+\sum r^{+}(y^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes x^{\prime}_{(1)})x_{(1)}^{\prime\prime}y_{(2)}^{\prime\prime}-\sum r^{+}(x^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes y^{\prime}_{(1)})y^{\prime\prime}_{(1)}x^{\prime\prime}_{(2)}.

(3)

The
G
action is given by the comodule map
\Delta_{G}:=(\mu_{G}\otimes id)\circ\Delta_{G\times G}
.

In the particular case where
X
is smooth, consider
X
as a smooth manifold and denote by
\pi_{X}
the Poisson bivector field defining the Poisson structure (i.e.
\{f,g\}(x)=\left<D_{x}f\otimes D_{x}g,\pi_{X,x}\right>
). Let
r^{-}:=\sigma(r^{+})
, where
\sigma(x\otimes y)=y\otimes x
. Let
a_{G\times G}:\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma(X,T_{X})
the infinitesimal action induced by the action of
G^{2}
on
X
. Then the fusion
X^{\circledast}
is the manifold
X
with the Poisson bivector field

\pi_{X^{\circledast}}=\pi_{X}+a_{G\times G}(r^{-}-r^{+}).

This is using this formula that the concept of fusion was introduced in the work of Alekseev-Malkin
[
AM95
]
. As we shall see, for a connected marked surface with non-trivial marking, then

\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is smooth. Recall the coaction
\Delta^{L}:\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]^{\otimes\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
induced by gluing some bigons to the boundary arcs. This endow
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
with a structure of
(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D})^{\mathcal{A}}
Poisson variety. In particular, by choosing two boundary arcs
a,b
, we get a structure of
(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D})^{2}
-Poisson variety on
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.
As a consequence of Costantino and Lê’s Theorem
2.20
, we get the

Theorem 4.19
.

If
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{a\circledast b}
is obtained from
\mathbf{\Sigma}
by fusioning
a
and
b
, then
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{a\circledast b})\cong\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{a\circledast b})
.

Proof.

Recall from Theorem
2.20
the isomorphism of vector spaces
\Psi_{a\circledast b}:\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\cong\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{a\circledast b})
so that the pull-back
\mu_{\circledast}
of the product in
\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{a\circledast b})
is the fusion, in the sense of Definition
2.19
, of the product
\mu
of
\mathcal{S}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. Denote by
\star_{\circledast}
and
\star
the products in
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\otimes\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
corresponding to
\mu_{\circledast}
and
\mu
respectively.
For
x,y\in\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
, we have

x\star_{\circledast}y=\sum\mathbf{r}_{\hbar}(y^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes x^{\prime}_{(1)})x^{\prime\prime}_{(1)}\star y^{\prime\prime}_{(2)},\quad\mbox{and}\quad y\star_{\circledast}x=\sum\mathbf{r}_{\hbar}(x^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes y^{\prime}_{(1)})y^{\prime\prime}_{(1)}\star x^{\prime\prime}_{(2)}.

Using the equalities
\mathbf{r}_{\hbar}(x\otimes y)\equiv\epsilon(x)\epsilon(y)+\hbar r^{+}(x\otimes y)\pmod{\hbar^{2}}
and
x\star y-y\star x=\hbar\{x,y\}\pmod{\hbar^{2}}
, we find:

x\star_{\circledast}y-y\star_{\circledast}x\equiv\hbar\left(\{x,y\}+\sum r^{+}(y^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes x^{\prime}_{(1)})x_{(1)}^{\prime\prime}y_{(2)}^{\prime\prime}-\sum r^{+}(x^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes y^{\prime}_{(1)})y^{\prime\prime}_{(1)}x^{\prime\prime}_{(2)}\right)\pmod{\hbar^{2}}.

∎

The main motivation for the authors of
[
AM95
]
to introduce the notion of fusion was to get the following decomposition (which is
[
AM95
, Theorem 2]
):

\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,n}^{0})\cong\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,0}^{0})^{\circledast g}\circledast(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS})^{\circledast n},

where
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,0}^{0})
is obtained from
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
by fusion. This reduces the study of
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,n}^{0})
to the study of
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
and
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
.
A useful concept, also introduced by Alekseev-Kosmann-Schwarzbach-Meinrenken in
[
AKSM02
]
is the

Definition 4.20
.

A smooth
G
-Poisson variety
X
is
non-degenerate
if the map

(a_{G},\pi^{\#}_{X}):\mathfrak{g}\oplus T_{X}^{*}\to T_{X}

is surjective, where
a_{G}:\mathfrak{g}\to T_{X}
is the infinitesimal
G
action and
\pi_{X}^{\#}
is the map induced from the Poisson bivector field
\pi_{X}
.

Lemma 4.21
.

( Ganev-Jordan-Safranov
[
GJSa
, Proposition
2.13
]
following
[
AKSM02
, Section
10
]
)
Let
X
a smooth
G^{2}\times H
-Poisson variety and
X^{\circledast}
is the
G\times H
-Poisson variety obtained by fusion. If
X
is non degenerate then
X^{\circledast}
is non-degenerate.

Lemma 4.22
.

For
g\geq 1
, the
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
-Poisson variety
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,0}^{0})
is non-degenerate.

Proof.

The fact that
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})=\mathcal{X}(\underline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{0,2})
is non-degenerate follows from Theorem
4.13
. By fusioning the two boundary arcs of
D_{+}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})
we obtain
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,0}^{0}
and by fusioning
g
copies of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1,0}^{0}
we obtain
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,0}^{0}
, so the lemma follows from Lemma
4.21
and Theorem
4.19
.
∎

Let
i:\mathbf{m}_{1}\to\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,0}^{0}
be the (unique up to isomorphism) embedding of marked surfaces illustrated in Figure
18
. Denote by
\mu_{q}:\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,n}^{0})
the induced algebra morphism and denote by
\mu:\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,0}^{0})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
the Poisson morphism induced by
\mu_{+1}
.

Figure 18.
The embedding
\mathbf{m}_{1}\to\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,0}^{0}
defining the moment map
\mu:\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,0}^{0})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}
.

The map
\mu
was called
Lie group valued moment map
in
[
AMM98
]
and the map
\mu_{q}
was named
quantum moment map
in
[
GJSa
]
. Note that
\mu_{q}
sends the unique bad arc of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{STS}]
to the unique bad arc of
\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{g,0}^{0})
, so the bad arcs decomposition

\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0})=\mathcal{X}^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0})\bigsqcup\mathcal{X}^{1}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0}),

is such that
\mathcal{X}^{i}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0})=\mu^{-1}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{i})
.

Theorem 4.23
.

(Ganev-Jordan-Safranov
[
GJSa
, Theorem
2.14
]
) Let
g\geq 1
. The open dense subset
\mathcal{X}^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0})\subset\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0})
is symplectic.

Proof.

Let
a:\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\to\Gamma({\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}}^{STS},T_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}})
be the infinitesimal action of the conjugacy action
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}\circlearrowright{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}}^{STS}
. By Theorem
4.17
, since the orbits of this action are symplectic, then the image of
a_{x}
is included in the image of
\pi_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0},x}^{\#}
for all
x\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{0}
, i.e. the infinitesimal action by conjugacy is Hamiltonian. Since
\mu
is Poisson and
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
equivariant, the infinitesimal action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{D}
on
\mathcal{X}^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0})
is also Hamiltonian. Moreover, since
\mathcal{X}^{0}(\underline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{g,n})
is non-degenerate, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0})
the map
\pi_{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0},x}^{\#}
is surjective, therefore
\mathcal{X}^{0}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{0}_{g,0})
is symplectic.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

In
[
SL91
, Theorem
5.9
]
, Sjamaar and Lerman proved that for any compact Lie group
G
, the relative character variety
\mathcal{X}_{G}(\underline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{g,n})
contains a connected open dense symplectic leaf and state that ”Although most of the results of this paper hold for proper actions of arbitrary Lie groups, the proofs are technically more difficult”. Theorem
4.23
is an explicit version of the case
G=\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and
n=1
.

4.4.
Relative character varieties

The Poisson variety
\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
admits a geometric interpretation that we now briefly describe and refer to
[
FR99
,
AM95
,
Kor19b
]
for details. Let
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
be the fundamental groupoid of
\Sigma
whose objects are points in
\Sigma
and morphisms are paths
\alpha:v_{0}\to v_{1}
. We keep the same notations than in Section
2.8
. The inclusion
\mathcal{A}\subset\Sigma
induces a fully-faithful functor
\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{A})\subset\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. The
representations space
is the set of functors

\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}):=\{\rho:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\operatorname{SL}_{2},|\rho(\alpha^{0})=\mathds{1}_{2},\forall\alpha^{0}\in\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{A})\}.

The
gauge group

\mathcal{G}
is the group of maps
g:\Sigma\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
with finite support such that
g(v)=\mathds{1}_{2}
for all
v\in\mathcal{A}
. It acts on
\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
by the formula

g\cdot\rho(\alpha)=g(t(\alpha))\rho(\alpha)g(s(\alpha))^{-1},\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{G},\rho\in\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\alpha\in\Pi_{1}(\Sigma).

The set
\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is the set of closed points of an affine scheme over
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C})
whose algebra of regular functions is

\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}]:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X_{\alpha,i,j}|\alpha\in\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),i,j=\pm]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left(M(\alpha)M(\beta)=M(\alpha\beta),\det(M(\alpha))=1,M(\alpha^{0})=\mathds{1}_{2},\mbox{ if }\alpha^{0}\in\Pi_{1}(\mathcal{A})\right)$}\right.},

where we wrote
M(\alpha):=\begin{pmatrix}X_{\alpha,+,+}&X_{\alpha,+,-}\\
X_{\alpha,-,+}&X_{\alpha,-,-}\end{pmatrix}
. Similarly, the elements of
\mathcal{G}
form the closed points of an affine group scheme over
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C})
whose Hopf algebra of regular functions is

\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{G}]={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}[X_{v,i,j}|v\in\Sigma,i,j=\pm]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left(\det(M(v))=1,M(v_{0})=\mathds{1}_{2}\mbox{ if }v_{0}\in\mathcal{A}\right)$}\right.},

where
M(v)=\begin{pmatrix}X_{v,+,+}&X_{v,+,-}\\
X_{v,-,+}&X_{v,-,-}\end{pmatrix}
.
Using the notation

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\boxtimes\begin{pmatrix}a^{\prime}&b^{\prime}\\
c^{\prime}&d^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a\otimes a^{\prime}+b\otimes c^{\prime}&a\otimes b^{\prime}+b\otimes d^{\prime}\\
c\otimes a^{\prime}+d\otimes c^{\prime}&c\otimes b^{\prime}+d\otimes d^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},

then the coproduct
\Delta
on
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{G}]
is given by
\Delta(M(v))=M(v)\boxtimes M(v)
and the antipode by
S(M(v)):=\begin{pmatrix}X_{v,-,-}&-X_{v,+,-}\\
X_{v,-,+}&X_{v,+,+}\end{pmatrix}
.
The action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is algebraic, given by the comodule map
\Delta^{L}(M(\alpha))=(\mu\otimes id)(id\otimes\tau)\left(M(t(\alpha))\boxtimes M(\alpha)\boxtimes S(M(s(\alpha)))\right)
.

Definition 4.25
.

The
relative character variety
is the algebraic quotient

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma}):=\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}.

Said differently, the algebra
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]
is defined as the set of coinvariant vectors for the coaction, i.e. as the kernel

0\to\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]\to\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]\xrightarrow{\Delta^{L}-\eta\circ\epsilon}\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{G}]\otimes\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})].

Curve functions
For
\gamma\in\Sigma
a simple closed curve, choose
v\in\gamma
and let
\gamma_{v}:v\to v
the path in
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
representing
\gamma
. Fix
f\in\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
a regular function invariant by conjugacy, i.e.
f=P(a+d)
is a polynomial in the trace function. Then the element
f_{\gamma}:=P(X_{\gamma_{v},+,+}+X_{\gamma_{v},-,-})\in\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]
is a coinvariant vector which only depends on the isotopy class of
\gamma
and not on its orientation (because
\operatorname{tr}(A^{-1})=\operatorname{tr}(A)
for
A\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}
). It corresponds to the function
f_{\gamma}([\rho])=P(\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma)))
. Similarly, for
g\in\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
an arbitrary function, so
g=P(a,b,c,d)
is a polynomial in the matrix coefficients
a,b,c,d\in\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
, and for
\alpha\subset\Sigma
a path with
s(\alpha),t(\alpha)\in\mathcal{A}
, the function
g_{\alpha}:=P(X_{\alpha,++},X_{\alpha,-+},X_{\alpha,-+},X_{\alpha,--})\in\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is a coinvariant vector which only depends on the isotopy class of
\alpha
relatively to
\mathcal{A}
(and depends on its orientation). So
g_{\alpha}([\rho])=g(\rho(\alpha))
. We call
curve functions
such functions
f_{\gamma},h_{\alpha}
and a theorem of Procesi
[
Pro87
]
implies that curves functions generate
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]
(see
[
Kor19b
]
for details).

Poisson structure
We now define some Poisson structures on
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
which depend on a choice
\mathfrak{o}
of orientations of the boundary arcs of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
. Let
f_{\alpha},g_{\beta}
be two curve functions and fix some geometric representatives of
\alpha
and
\beta
which only intersect transversally in the interior of
\Sigma
and let us introduce some notations. Fix
\rho\in\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
. Recall the notations introduced in Section
3.2
while defining the relative intersection form. In particular for
v\in\alpha\cap\beta
we have defined a sign
\epsilon(v)
depending whether the tangent vectors of
\alpha
and
\beta
at
v
form an oriented bases of
T_{v}\Sigma
or not. Also
for
a
a boundary arc, we write
S(a):=a\cap\alpha\times a\cap\beta
and for
(v_{1},v_{2})\in S(a)
, we have defined a sign
\epsilon(v_{1},v_{2})=\pm 1
in the same manner.
Define
\mathfrak{o}(v_{1},v_{2})=+1
if
v_{1}<_{\mathfrak{o}}v_{2}
and
\mathfrak{o}(v_{1},v_{2})=-1
else.
Let
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right):\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\otimes\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
be the non degenerate Ad-invariant symmetric form normalized so that its dual is the symmetric part
\frac{1}{2}H\otimes H
of
r^{+}
.
Let
X_{f,\alpha}\in\mathfrak{\sl{}_{2}}
be the unique vector satisfying
(X_{f,\alpha},Y)=D_{\rho(\alpha)}f(Y)
. For
v
a point in
\alpha
, decompose the path in
\alpha=\alpha^{+}\alpha^{-}
where
s(\alpha^{+})=t(\alpha^{-})=v
and write
X_{f,\alpha}(v):=\rho(\alpha^{+})^{-1}X_{f,\alpha}\rho(\alpha^{-})^{-1}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. Extend
(\cdot,\cdot)
to a bilinear form on
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{\otimes 2}
by
(x\otimes y,z\otimes t):=(x,z)(y,t)
.

Definition 4.26
.

The Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{\mathfrak{o}}
on
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]
is defined by the following generalized Goldman formula

\displaystyle\{f_{\alpha},g_{\beta}\}^{\mathfrak{o}}([\rho])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{(v_{1},v_{2})\in S(a)}\varepsilon(v_{1},v_{2})\left(X_{f,\alpha}(v_{1})\otimes X_{g,\beta}(v_{2}),r^{\mathfrak{o}(v_{1},v_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{v\in c_{1}\cap c_{2}}\varepsilon(v)\left(X_{f,\alpha}(v),X_{g,\beta}(v)\right)

That the Poisson bracket of two regular functions is a regular function and the fact that
\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{\mathfrak{o}}
satisfies the Jacobi identity are non trivial facts.

Discrete models
The relative character variety is an algebraic variety, i.e.
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})]
is reduced and finitely generated. To prove this, fix
\mathbb{V}\subset\Sigma
a finite subset such that
(1)

\mathbb{V}
intersects each boundary arc
a
once, in say
v_{a}
, and
(2)

\mathbb{V}
intersects each connected component of
\Sigma
at least once. Let
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})\subset\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
the full subcategory generated by
\mathbb{V}
and define a finite presentation
\mathbb{P}=(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{RL})
of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{V})
as in Definition
2.28
. Write
\mathbb{RL}=(R_{1},\ldots,R_{m})
and consider the
R_{j}
as Laurent monomials in the generators
\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{G}
. Define the algebraic map
\mathcal{R}:(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\mathbb{G}}\to(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\mathbb{RL}}
by
\mathcal{R}=(R_{1},\ldots,R_{m})
. The
discrete representations space
is the affine subvariety
\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\mathbb{P})\subset(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\mathbb{G}}
of elements
\rho
such that
\mathcal{R}(\rho)=(\mathds{1}_{2},\ldots,\mathds{1}_{2})
. Write
\mathring{\mathbb{V}}\subset\mathbb{V}
the subset of elements which are not in
\mathcal{A}
and define the
discrete gauge group

\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{V}}:=(\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\mathring{\mathbb{V}}}\cong\{g:\mathbb{V}\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}|g(v_{a})=\mathds{1}_{2},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\}
. It acts on
\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\mathbb{P})
algebraically by the same formula

g\cdot\rho(\beta)=g(t(\beta))\rho(\beta)g(s(\beta))^{-1},\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{V}},\rho\in\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\mathbb{P}),\beta\in\mathbb{G}.

The
discrete character variety
is the algebraic quotient

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\mathbb{P}):=\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\mathbb{P})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{V}}.

Now consider the algebraic morphisms
Res:\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\mathbb{P})
and
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{V}}
sending
\rho\in\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
to its restriction to
\mathbb{G}
and sending
g\in\mathcal{G}
to its restriction to
\mathbb{V}
. The morphism
Res
is clearly
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{V}}
equivariant for the induced structure so it defines a morphism

\Psi_{\mathbb{P}}:\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma},\mathbb{P}).

Theorem 4.27
.

(K.
[
Kor19b
, Proposition
2.5
]
)
\Psi_{\mathbb{P}}
is an isomorphism.

In particular,
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is an algebraic variety.

Character varieties for closed surfaces

Let us now consider the case where
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma,\emptyset)
is closed and choose
\mathbb{V}=\{v\}
a singleton. Then Theorem
4.27
shows that

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma,\emptyset)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v),\operatorname{SL}_{2})\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\operatorname{SL}_{2},

where
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
acts by conjugacy. Under this form, character varieties were introduced by Culler and Shalen in
[
CS83
]
as a powerful tool to analyse
3
-manifolds, in particular to study their incompressible surfaces. Suppose that
\Sigma
is closed and connected.
The action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
on
\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v),\operatorname{SL}_{2})
by conjugacy is not free.

Definition 4.28
.

We decompose the set of representations
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}
into three classes:

(1)

The
central representations
taking value in
\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
and for which the stabilizer is
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
.

(2)

The
diagonal representations
which are conjugate to a non central representation valued in the subgroup
D\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}
of diagonal matrices and for which the stabilizer is the group of diagonal matrices.

(3)

The
irreducible representations
for which the stabilizer is
\pm\mathds{1}_{2}
.

Accordingly, we decompose the (closed points of the) character variety in three classes

\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma,\emptyset)=\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{0}(\Sigma)\bigsqcup\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{1}(\Sigma)\bigsqcup\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{2}(\Sigma),

where
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{i}(\Sigma)
is the set of classes of irreducible, diagonal and central representations when
i=0,1,2
respectively.

When
\Sigma
has genus
1
, the set
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{0}(\Sigma)
is empty and the smooth locus of the character variety is
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{1}(\Sigma)
. When
\Sigma
has genus
g\geq 2
, the smooth locus is
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{0}(\Sigma)
. The Poisson variety
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{diag}(\Sigma)=\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{1}(\Sigma)\bigsqcup\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{2}(\Sigma)
is easy to study. Consider the quadratic pair
\mathbb{E}=(\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}),(\cdot,\cdot))
formed by the first homology group with the intersection form and denote by
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma):=\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{E})\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{C}^{*})
the associated symplectic torus (which is the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
character variety). The diagonal inclusion
\varphi:\mathbb{C}^{*}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}
, sending
z
to
\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&z^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
, induces a morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{diag}(\Sigma)
sending
\chi:\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
to the representation
\Phi(\chi)
sending
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,v)
to
\varphi(\chi[\gamma])
. Clearly
\Phi
is a double covering which is branched along the set
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{2}(\Sigma)
of central representations.

In algebraic language,
\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)]=\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})]
and for
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to D
a diagonal representation, the value
\tau_{\gamma}(\rho)=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma))
only depends on the homology class
[\gamma]\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})]
.
The morphism
\Phi^{*}:\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}^{diag}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma,\emptyset)]\to\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)]
sends a trace function
\tau_{\gamma}
to the homology class
[\gamma]
. It is an immediate consequence of Goldman formula in Definition
4.26
that for
\rho\in\mathrm{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
a functor valued in the subset of diagonal matrices, then

\{\tau_{[\alpha]},\tau_{[\beta]}\}(\rho)=\sum_{v\in\alpha\cap\beta}\epsilon(v)\tau_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\rho)=([\alpha],[\beta])\tau_{[\alpha]+[\beta]}(\rho).

Therefore the double branched covering
\Phi:\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{diag}(\Sigma)
is Poisson and since
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\Sigma)
is symplectic we have proved the

Lemma 4.29
.

The leaf
\mathcal{X}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}^{1}(\Sigma)
is symplectic.

Atiyah and Bott revisited in
[
AB83
]
character varieties in the language of gauge theory by studying 2 dimensional Yang-Mills theory. Let
\Omega_{F}^{1}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
denote the set of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
valued
1
-forms
A
on
\Sigma
with vanishing curvature, i.e. such that
FA:=dA+\frac{1}{2}[A\wedge A]=0
. Let
\mathcal{G}=C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\operatorname{SL}_{2})
act on
\Omega_{F}^{1}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
by
A^{g}=g^{-1}Ag+g^{-1}dg
and write

\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\Omega_{F}^{1}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{sl}_{2})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{G}$}\right.}.

The set
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\Sigma)
is isomorphic to the moduli space of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
-flat structures on
\Sigma
, i.e. the set of isomorphism classes of pairs
(E,\nabla)
where
E\to\Sigma
is a principal
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
-bundle and
\nabla
a flat connection. Since
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})=\pi_{2}(\operatorname{SL}_{2})=0
, such a bundle is always isomorphic to the trivial bundle
\Sigma\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}
in which
\nabla=d+A
for
A\in\Omega_{F}^{1}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. The group
\mathcal{G}
then identifies with the group of automorphisms of the trivial bundle so the correspondance is clear. By imposing some Sobolev regularity to the
1
-forms
A
